"selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "knowledge/algorithms/speculation-budget.md", "sha256": "3e4cc0caa43080c8e9b7b56de4b61dda72da5cd8e51a650f342311396c7091a3", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:7563]\n# K-ALG-006：投机解码：分布保持、状态提交与收益模型\n\n投机解码用较便宜的过程提出多个候选，再由目标模型并行验证，把单条请求的部分串行步骤换成并行宽度。收益来自每轮确认多个输出；代价包括草稿、未采用的验证计算、状态恢复、额外驻留和对其他请求的挤占。决定后续历史的是确认路径，物理写入过缓存并不等于可以提交。\n\n## 问题、分布契约与状态\n\n设已确认前缀为 H，目标在前缀 h 下规定的下一 token 分布为 p(·|h)，实际草稿提议分布为 q(·|h)。链式回合的名义候选预算为 γ≥0；未截短时产生 x₁,…,xγ，并取得 γ+1 个条件分布：pᵢ=p(·|H+x₁:ᵢ₋₁)，qᵢ=q(·|H+x₁:ᵢ₋₁)。首次拒绝前接受 n 个候选时，输出 x₁:ₙ 加一个修正 token；全部接受且未终止时，再从末端分布产生 bonus token。无提前停止时，回合产出 ℓ=n+1。遇到终止、输出预算或目标非法草稿后缀时，实际候选、输入行和所需分布分别截短，不能继续把它们都计为 γ。\n\n输入包括目标与草稿各自的模型、adapter、数值配置、采样变换，确认前缀及模型状态，γ 或树预算、grammar 状态、随机流和输出预算。输出包括确认 token、共同提交后的状态、临时对象的回收责任，以及接受长度和成本观测。\n\n分布契约需要区分两种一致性：\n\n- pᵢ 必须来自规定的目标采样契约；qᵢ 必须准确描述实际产生候选的提议过程。二者分别绑定各自稳定的模型、adapter、精度和变换，不能在生成候选后用另一个 q 计算接受率。\n- pᵢ、qᵢ 对应同一逻辑条件前缀，并在可比较的 token 空间上定义。相同模型、精度、temperature 或截断方式均为可选配置，不是分布保持的必要条件。不同 tokenizer 需要先建立明确的提议及概率映射，不能直接比较同编号 token。\n\n目标的 temperature、惩罚、top-k/top-p、grammar 及其顺序共同定义 p；草稿可以采用不同变换，但接受与残余修正必须使用变换后的实际 q。已确认且尚未终止的前缀若没有可归一化的目标合法质量，应进入规定的失败或恢复分支，不能静默抽取非法 token。草稿提出目标概率为零的 token 可以合法发生，接受概率为零即可；其后的草稿前缀不可因此被要求也具有目标分布。\n\n请求状态至少区分：已确认 token 序列及长度 L；所有必要层状态共同有效的输入消费边界 K；物理预留和存活对象的容量 A。全 attention 模型中，K 表示前 K 个 token 的全层 KV 可见；递归模型则要求对应位置的完整递归、卷积和窗口状态。通常 0≤K≤L。一个新输出 token 可能已经 known，却尚未作为模型输入，因此没有自己的 KV。\n\n容量 A 按物理对象并集计字节，不能与 token 长度直接比较。共享前缀只计一份；预留池及池内对象不能重复计量；在途读者仍占用的拒绝页也不能提前扣除。\n\n正确性要求同时保持目标输出语义、条件前缀、位置与行映射，以及 KV/递归状态、grammar、输出游标的共同提交边界。随机流还要保证提议、接受和重采样所需的独立性；不能把随机数计数简单等同于已输出 token 数。\n\n## 接受、残余修正与支持集\n\n以下推导在精确概率算术下成立。设 p、q 是同一有限 token 空间上的任意归一化分布。先提出 x∼q，对实际提出的候选计算\n\n a(x)=min(1,p(x)/q(x))。\n\n候选来自 q，所以求比值的位置必有 q(x)>0。若记录的 q(x)=0，却声称该候选由 q 抽得，说明提议记录与实际过程不一致；这与允许词表其他位置 q(u)=0 是两回事。\n\n令 β=Σᵤmin(p(u),q(u))，Z=Σᵤ[p(u)−q(u)]₊=1−β。拒绝时从完整 token 空间上的残余分布\n\n r(u)=[p(u)−q(u)]₊/Z\n\n抽取修正 token。接受分支给 u 的质量为 min(p(u),q(u))；拒绝分支给它的质量为 Zr(u)=[p(u)−q(u)]₊，两者之和正是 p(u)。因此不要求 q 覆盖 p 的正支持。q(u)=0 且 p(u)>0 的质量通过残余分支恢复，不能把残余归一化范围截成草稿候选集合。\n\n反例可以直接算清：p=(1/2,1/2)、q=(1,0)。草稿总提出 a，以 1/2 接受；拒绝概率为 1/2，残余分布必选 b，最终仍为 p。确定性 lookup 的单点提议或截断草稿因此不会仅因支持较小而非法。\n\n两个边界进一步区分该协议与普通拒绝采样。互斥支持时 β=0，所有候选被拒绝，r=p，仍保持分布，但没有候选接受收益。p=q 时 β=1，拒绝事件概率为零，不应计算或采样除以零的残余分布。有限精度实现若在此时进入拒绝分支，需要检查舍入、概率记录或采样比较，不能以任意 fallback 掩盖不一致。\n\ngreedy 可直接逐位比较目标 argmax：匹配则保留，首次不匹配输出目标 argmax，全部匹配则从末端分布取 bonus。目标平局规则、禁用 token 和停止规则必须固定。temperature=0 应有明确的 greedy 定义，不能通过除以零实现。也可把确定性 argmax 表达为单点概率分布，但实现必须保留同样的平局语义。\n\n精确分布等价不保证相同 seed 下文本逐 token 相同。提议、接受和重采样改变随机数消耗路径；seed 不能单独保证跨执行路径、平台或版本逐位一致。真实并行前向还可能改变浮点归约顺序：应声明目标参考函数及数值合同，不能把精确概率证明当作任意低精度实现的逐位证明。\n\n## 输入行、可见 KV 与输出 logits\n\n必须分别回答四个问题：本行输入哪个 token；本行读取哪些 KV；本行输出哪个条件分布；验证一个候选应该取哪一行。\n\n令 H 长 m，root 表示确认前缀末行。链式候选 xᵢ 的输入行位于逻辑位置 m+i−1，读取确认前缀和 x₁,…,xᵢ，包含自身 KV；该行输出 pᵢ₊₁ 所需的原始 logits，而不是 pᵢ。验证 x₁ 用 root 的 logits，验证 xᵢ（i>1）用 xᵢ₋₁ 行的 logits。未截短时，xγ 行提供全部接受后的 bonus 分布所需 logits。原始 logits 的产生与按某个前缀形成合法概率分布是两个阶段；后者只在该条件前缀可达且需要继续采样时执行。\n\n树中令 A(v) 为不含 v 的严格祖先路径，row(v) 为候选输入行。该行的合法可见集合是 H∪A(v)∪{v}，逻辑位置为 m+depth(v)−1。它输出 p(·|H+A(v)+v) 所需 logits。验证 v 则取父节点行的输出；若父节点是 root，就取确认前缀末行的输出。兄弟、堂兄弟和后代均不可见。不同分支同深度可以具有相同逻辑位置，物理行号不等于位置编码输入。\n\n以下逐层关系解释为什么屏蔽自身 key 无法把节点行变成父行。以常见的 pre-norm decoder block 为例，候选 v 的初始 hᵥ⁰ 来自 token embedding 和该模型的位置规则。第 l 层先从 hᵥˡ 经归一化、投影和位置处理得到 Qᵥˡ、Kᵥˡ、Vᵥˡ；历史 KV 来自此前在同层计算的相应前缀状态。对可见集合 M(v) 计算\n\n sᵥᵤ=Qᵥˡ·Kᵤˡ/√d，wᵥᵤ=softmaxᵤ∈M(v)(sᵥᵤ)，aᵥ=Σᵤ∈M(v)wᵥᵤVᵤˡ。\n\n随后经过输出投影、残差以及模型规定的归一化和 MLP，得到下一层输入 hᵥˡ⁺¹。各候选在每一层先从该层输入产生自己的 Q/K/V，再按可见集合计算输出；下一层消费这些输出。不能把某层祖先的最终 hidden 当作所有层的 KV。不可见位置应从归一化集合排除或采用等价加性遮罩，仅将 score 乘零不能保证概率为零。\n\n即使排除自身 K/V，节点 v 的 embedding、query 和残差仍含 v；同时遗漏自身还破坏正常 causal attention。因此修复 logits 偏移应改父行索引，不能修改 mask 来伪造父前缀。完成全部必要层后，末层 hidden 经模型规定的 final norm 与 LM head 生成下一位置 logits，再在需要该分布时按对应父前缀应用目标采样变换。\n\n这也固定了未截短回合的计算口径：若 root logits 与全部 H 的状态已有效，γ 个链候选只需新增 γ 个模型输入行，却可取得 γ+1 个条件分布。若上一轮新输出的末 token 尚无状态，则还要计算该 root 输入行，新增行为 γ+1。树有 N 个非 root 候选时对应 N 或 N+1 行。已有完整 KV 却缺少兼容 root logits 时，可在私有写区使用 H 的前 m−1 个状态重算末 token；不能重复追加它或覆盖共享页。缺失更长状态后缀时，应先按已知 token 完成必要 prefill。约束路径截断后的实际输入行数按下一节的 s 计量。\n\n## 链式回合与共同提交\n\n下述算法把 root 准备与候选事务分开。root 准备只消费已经确认的 token，可以独立推进 K；候选计算的状态在接受决定前保持临时。\n\n先区分三种状态。已确认前缀本身没有合法延伸且请求尚未终止，是目标生成合同的真实失败。草稿某个 token 不被目标 grammar 允许，则它在合法父前缀下的目标概率为零，必被拒绝；它之后的 grammar 死前缀不需要目标分布。grammar 已接受当前文本也不必等于终止：例如允许 a 与 ab 时，在 a 后仍可继续；只有 EOS 或声明的实际停止规则结束生成。\n\n本章选择先检查目标 grammar 路径、截短模型输入，再延迟形成目标概率分布。路径检查完整消费每个候选的 token 展开，保留仍可完成的解析配置；不只是检查首字符。首次非法候选 j 仍保留 xⱼ 与实际 qⱼ，用其父分布 pⱼ 验证和修正，但不把 xⱼ 或其后缀送入目标前向。于是只需输入 x₁:ⱼ₋₁；pⱼ 来自 root 或 xⱼ₋₁ 行。合法终止候选则保留自身输入行，在它之后停止准备；这是便于提交该候选全层状态的选择，不能再消费其后继分布。非法判定先于终止判定，非法 EOS 也不能直接结束目标请求。\n\n令 g≤γ 为实际生成的草稿数，c≤g 为保留到首次非法候选、目标终止候选或列表末尾的扫描长度。若第 c 个候选非法，目标候选输入数 s=c−1；否则 s=c。即使原始模型可以对任意 token 串产生 logits，也不能因此对 grammar 死前缀归一化。对 grammar 合法但被目标其他变换置零的候选，同样在接受扫描时拒绝，不再构造其后继目标分布。\n\n```pseudocode\n算法 链式投机一轮\n输入：非空确认前缀 H，目标/草稿各自的配置，状态 S，候选预算 γ≥0，剩余输出预算 M≥0\n输出：确认输出、共同状态、待回收对象\n\n准备 root：\n  若输入为空，按模型合同补 BOS 或拒绝请求\n  若 M=0 或请求已终止，直接结束，不准备后续分布或候选\n  用已确认 token 补算缺失的目标状态后缀\n  若状态完整但缺少兼容 root logits：\n    使用 H 去掉末 token 的历史，在私有写区重算末 token\n  等全部必要层完成后，保留 H 的完整状态和 root logits\n  保存共同起点 S0；此时 K=|H|\n  用 H 的正式 grammar 与目标变换形成 p₁，并保留以供复用\n  若该非终止前缀没有可归一化的目标合法质量：返回真实失败，不产生输出\n\n草稿：\n  candidates、q_records ← 空；草稿私有状态从 H 准备\n  对 i=1..min(γ,M)：\n    若草稿已终止或合法提议过程没有后继：停止本轮提议\n    h ← H + 已生成候选\n    用草稿自己的配置与私有状态产生实际 qᵢ\n    校验 qᵢ 非负、归一化且可在共同 token 空间解释\n    从 qᵢ 抽取 xᵢ\n    记录 xᵢ 及足以恢复该次完整 qᵢ 的信息\n    推进草稿私有状态；不得推进正式输出或正式 grammar\n    若 xᵢ 触发草稿自己的终止规则：立即停止，不构造后续 q\n  g ← 实际候选数；γ=0 或无提议时允许 g=0\n  数值错误、错误身份或伪造的 q 不属于正常的提议停止\n\n检查目标路径：\n  G ← S0 的目标 grammar 私有副本；c←0；s←0；末候选非法←假\n  依次检查实际候选 xᵢ：\n    c ← i\n    若完整目标 grammar 转移非法或转移后无可完成配置：\n      标记 xᵢ 在合法父状态 G 下的目标概率为零\n      保留 (xᵢ,qᵢ) 和父状态 G；末候选非法←真\n      不建立它的后继 grammar，不检查其后缀；停止路径检查\n    保存合法转移后的私有 grammar 状态；G←该状态；s←i\n    若该合法候选触发目标 EOS、实际停止规则或输出预算：\n      标记目标终止边界；停止路径检查，不准备后继状态\n  无候选时 c=s=0\n  其余草稿候选仅为已生成但不会被目标消费的临时对象\n\n目标前向：\n  只将 x₁:s 的 s 个输入行放入临时区\n  每层为 xᵢ 构造 H + x₁:ᵢ 的可见集合，包含自身\n  按逐层 hidden→Q/K/V→attention→下一层 hidden 计算\n  保存所需原始 logits 或可恢复 logits 的 hidden，不预先变换全部后缀\n  验证 x₁ 的来源为 root；验证 xᵢ 的来源为 xᵢ₋₁ 行\n  即使 x_c 非法，验证它所需的父行仍然存在；不需要 x_c 输入行\n  等待完成证据覆盖实际执行行的必要层、状态及所需输出\n  实际执行失败、身份失效或已取消时：不发布候选结果，保留 S0 并排空使用者\n\n接受：\n  n←0；t←不存在；接受候选终止←假\n  对 i=1..c：\n    此时只有此前候选全部接受，才到达当前父前缀\n    i=1 时复用 p₁；否则按该父前缀的目标变换和合法 grammar 形成 pᵢ\n    可达且非终止父状态若无合法质量：按目标合同返回真实失败\n    确认记录的 qᵢ(xᵢ)>0\n    U←与提议及此前接受判断独立的均匀随机数，取值于 [0,1)\n    若 U < min(1,pᵢ(xᵢ)/qᵢ(xᵢ))：\n      n←n+1\n      若该候选触发目标终止或输出预算：\n        接受候选终止←真；停止扫描，不产生 bonus\n    否则：\n      在完整 token 空间形成 R=norm([pᵢ−qᵢ]₊)\n      该可达拒绝分支要求残余总质量大于零\n      从 R 抽取 t；停止扫描，不构造 pᵢ₊₁ 或更后分布\n  若没有拒绝且没有接受候选终止：\n    n=0 时复用 p₁；否则从 xₙ 行形成其后继目标分布\n    从该分布抽取 bonus t；不为 t 准备后继分布\n\n提交：\n  known ← H + x₁:ₙ + t（若存在 t）\n  从所有必要层选取恰好消费 H + x₁:ₙ 后的状态\n  对 append-only KV，逻辑有效边界 K ← |H|+n\n  对递归/窗口状态，恢复该边界的快照或从共同起点重放\n  grammar 从起点沿确认输出推进，输出游标推进相同 token 序列\n  根据确认输出记录实际终止原因；草稿私有终止不自动终止目标请求\n  提交本轮随机流计数与请求代次，保持后续随机数独立\n  t 若存在，已确认但尚未作为输入；此时 K=|known|−1\n  若接受候选自身终止且无 t，则 K=|known|\n  其余候选状态立即失去逻辑可见性，最后使用者结束后才回收物理空间\n```\n\n草稿停止只结束本轮提议，不取消目标校正。草稿在自己的约束下无后继时，可以用已经生成的 g 个候选继续验证；g=0 退化为直接目标采样。草稿自己的终止 token 若在目标下非法，应被拒绝；若在目标下合法但并不触发目标停止，全部候选接受后仍可产生 bonus。停止规则、EOS 是否占逻辑输出预算及是否显示为文本，由目标输出合同固定，不能由草稿决定。\n\n一个确定性反例可以验证顺序。取合法 H，目标 grammar 只允许下一个 token 为 a，因此 p₁=(1,0)；草稿无此约束，q₁=(0,1)，γ=2，已经提出 b,b。目标路径检查在首个 b 截断，得到 g=2、c=1、s=0。p₁ 来自 root，a₁(b)=0；用严格比较 U<0 必然拒绝。完整残余为 norm((1,0)−(0,1))₊=(1,0)，输出 a。known 变为 H+a，K 仍为 |H|，grammar 只确认 a；两个 b 都不能发布。不构造或消费 p(·|H+b)，因此该死前缀无法阻止可达的修正分支。若先对全部草稿后缀做 grammar 归一化，就会在输出 a 之前失败，这是必须被检查拒绝的错误变体。\n\nγ=0 退化为从 root 分布产生一个普通目标 token。n+1 产出适用于存在修正或 bonus 的回合；若已接受候选本身触发终止，产出为 n，不再加一。接受的终止候选在本算法中已有全层输入状态，可以 K=L；从修正或 bonus 抽出的终止 token 仍未作为输入，可以 K=L−1。若请求已经结束且只需返回输出，没有必要为后者补算 KV。\n\n设 H 长 5，候选为 x₁、x₂、x₃，root logits 已保存且目标路径未提前截断。新增输入行为 3：x₁ 行产生 p₂ 所需 logits，x₂ 行产生 p₃ 所需 logits，x₃ 行产生 p₄ 所需 logits。若只接受前两个，用 x₂ 行形成 p₃，与 q₃ 修正得到 t。提交后 known 长 8，K=7；保留 x₁、x₂ 的全层 KV，丢弃 x₃，t 等下一轮输入。将 K 写成 8 会跳过 t 的模型计算；提交 x₃ 则会读取错误分支。下一轮通常需计算 t 这\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 8000:17437]\n次完成不能重新获得提交权；物理页、图缓冲和传输源须等待各自最后读者结束。共享前缀的可变尾页需要私有写区或写时复制。\n\n## 树形验证与可实现的选择协议\n\n树 attention 只负责同时计算各条路径的目标状态，不自动证明路径选择保持分布。对每个孩子独立执行链式接受公式，再任选一条通过路径，通常会改变输出概率。树还需规定候选生成、顺序、重复候选、拒绝后的分布更新以及回退规则。\n\n下面给出一个完整且保守的协议：将已建好的每个父节点的孩子看作有序的、互异的确定性提议，每次提议的 q 是该 token 的单点分布。树可以来自草稿模型或 lookup，但验证随机数独立于建树随机数；孩子顺序不能根据尚未消费的验证随机数挑选。本协议是本章的推导，用于明确一种合法实现，不声称等同于 EAGLE 的具体重采样实现。\n\n在父路径下令初始工作分布 r=p。依序尝试孩子 token c：以 r(c) 接受；若拒绝，将 c 的质量删除并重新归一化，即 r←norm([r−δc]₊)。若所有孩子都拒绝，从最终 r 抽一个 token 并结束本轮；若接受孩子，则进入该孩子，使用其输入行输出的下一位置目标分布继续。叶子没有孩子，直接从叶子行分布抽 bonus。\n\n证明可按固定孩子顺序展开。到达第 j 个孩子前的生存质量为 1−Σₖ<ⱼp(cₖ)，当前 r(cⱼ)=p(cⱼ)/(1−Σₖ<ⱼp(cₖ))，因此在该孩子处输出 cⱼ 的无条件质量是 p(cⱼ)。全部拒绝后的质量为未列孩子集合的目标总质量，乘最终残余分布正好恢复这些 token 的 p。拒绝概率为零的分支不可进入。每一父路径上都恢复目标条件分布，递归后便恢复整条输出序列的目标分布。\n\n```pseudocode\n算法 树形目标前向与有序单点提议验证\n输入：H、root logits、有限候选树 V、父指针、每父节点的孩子顺序\n输出：确认路径、最终修正/bonus token、共同状态\n\n检查树：\n  父指针必须无环且连到 root\n  同一父节点下按 token 去重，保留规定顺序\n  建立 node→输入行、父节点→logits 行、逻辑深度→位置的映射\n  按目标 grammar 检查各父路径；非法边的后代不准备后继 grammar 或目标分布\n  合法终止节点不扩展；非法孩子仍可在合法父分布下以零概率接受\n\n目标前向：\n  对保留的候选输入节点 v，允许读取 H、严格祖先及自身的 KV\n  每层从本层输入 hidden 产生所有保留候选的 Q/K/V\n  用构造的可见集合实际执行 attention，产生下一层输入\n  经所有必要层与 LM head 得到每节点之后的原始 logits\n  等全层状态与所需输出共同完成；保持所有候选状态临时\n\n选择：\n  u ← root；path ← 空\n  循环：\n    只有当前父路径可达且未终止，才应用该路径的目标变换形成 r\n    chosen ← 空\n    按顺序遍历 u 的孩子 v：\n      c ← token(v)\n      U ← 新的独立均匀随机数，取值于 [0,1)\n      若 U < r(c)：chosen ← v；停止孩子循环\n      否则：\n        r ← norm([r−δc]₊)\n        此拒绝事件若可达，归一化总质量必须大于零\n    若 chosen 为空：\n      t ← 从 r 抽样；停止选择循环\n    path 追加 chosen\n    若 chosen 的 token 触发终止或输出预算：结束且不抽额外 token\n    u ← chosen\n\n提交：\n  从所有必要层共同选取 path 对应的状态及路径顺序\n  known ← H + path 的 token + t（若存在 t）\n  t 若存在则尚未作为输入，不具有自己的 KV\n  恢复并提交同路径 grammar、随机协议状态和输出\n  未保留节点及后代失去可见性；最后使用者结束后回收\n```\n\n例如 root 有孩子 A、B，A 有孩子 C。A、B 的输入行都读取 H 和自身；C 读取 H、A、C。A 与 B 都由 root 分布验证，C 由 A 行分布验证。若路径为 A、C，则 C 行提供后续分布。全层提交只收集 A、C 的状态，不能因物理行连续而收集 A、B。\n\n上述树协议不消费原始草稿 q 的概率，只把树作为确定性候选列表。若要利用其他多候选提议协议取得更高接受收益，必须另行解释其实际提议分布及逐次残余更新，不能将本协议的证明移用于任意 top-k 树。\n\n## 草稿来源与条件化成本\n\n独立小模型增加草稿权重加载、驻留及可能的跨设备传输；n-gram 或 prompt lookup 将草稿降为查找和复制，但匹配、建树、概率记录及验证仍有成本，不能严格称为零成本。确定性 lookup 可以用单点 q，由完整残余修正保持目标分布，接受收益取决于目标给该候选的概率。\n\nEAGLE v1 在 LM head 前的目标特征上进行草稿自回归，并输入提前一步的已采样 token，使下一特征预测能区分采样造成的不同路径。它以树 attention 验证候选，并在各层递归应用采样或分布修正。该来源支持特征草稿和树预算的机制，不取消父行 logits、实际分布和共同提交要求。其链式接受率与树形产出不能混用；树处理更多 token，MoE 验证还可能触及更多专家权重。\n\n设 D(γ)、V(γ)、H(γ) 分别为同一回合的草稿、目标验证及管理成本，单位为时间。若这些阶段在该测量范围内串行，单位确认 token 时间约为 (D+V+H)/E[ℓ]；若存在重叠，应使用实际依赖关键路径，不能把重叠时间直接相加。只有在相同请求集合、质量、batch 条件与 SLO 下优于普通目标解码，才构成收益。\n\n在固定 γ、没有提前停止的范围内，不依赖 i.i.d. 假设的产出恒等式为\n\n E[ℓ]=1+Σᵢ₌₁^γP(n≥i)。\n\n若各步接受事件可用独立、固定接受率 α 近似，则 E[ℓ]=Σᵢ₌₀^γαⁱ=(1−α^(γ+1))/(1−α)，α=1 时为 γ+1。真实接受事件依赖前缀和草稿误差，应记录接受长度分布与逐深度生存概率。EOS、输出上限及短请求会截短产出，不能继续无条件套用长生成公式。对包含提前停止的本章状态机，可统一写 ℓ=n+Iₜ，Iₜ 表示是否实际输出了修正或 bonus；据实际分布取期望，不再强行令 Iₜ=1。\n\n论文的简化墙钟模型设一次验证与一个普通目标步耗时相同、草稿每步耗时为目标的 c 倍，得到 S=E[ℓ]/(1+cγ)。在该模型中 α>c 时 γ=1 已有收益，但不意味着更大的 γ 总更好。真实模型应改用 S=E[ℓ]/(d(γ)+v(γ)+h(γ))，各项均以同条件普通目标步时间归一化。\n\n算术量需按输入行重新列账。令 B 为每个目标输入行的近似算术量，R 为本回合补算 root 的行数，则未截短链式目标新增量约为 (γ+R)B，另加草稿和 attention 随长度变化的部分。R=0 表示已保存 root logits，典型跨回合状态 R=1；树将 γ 换成实际输入节点数。论文在其 γ+1 次目标求值口径下得到算术量因子 (γĉ+γ+1)/E[ℓ]，ĉ 为草稿与目标每步算术量比。不能把这个公式误写为“已有 root logits 时仍需 γ+1 个新候选输入行”，也不能在 R=0 的局部回合里漏掉产生 root 的前次工作。\n\n路径截断后，草稿按实际 g 个提议列账，目标新增输入按 s+R 行列账，目标概率变换按实际到达并消费的父分布数列账，root 的 p₁ 形成后复用。首个目标非法候选 j 对应 s=j−1；本算法保留合法终止候选自身输入行，所以终止位置 τ 对应 s=τ，但不归一化其后继分布。此前已经生成的草稿后缀仍付草稿成本；已经提交执行的额外模型行或图 padding 也必须照实计费，不能因后来丢弃而从记录中删除。若选择另一种提前提交全部原始行、只延迟概率变换的实现，语义可保持，但输入行、KV、完成等待和峰值容量须按那条实际路径重新计算。\n\n| 类别 | 同一候选上的成本与条件 |\n|---|---|\n| 计算 | 实际 g 个草稿提议；s+R 或实际树节点数加 R 个目标输入行；attention、LM head、实际消费分布的接受与残余归一化。目标已饱和时额外行会挤占其他请求。 |\n| 访存 | 目标权重可能在多行间复用；每行仍读其可见 KV，并写临时候选 KV。链中 s 行的可见对数为 sm+s(s+1)/2，树中为实际输入节点上的 Σᵥ(m+depth(v))，均是逻辑配对数，不等于 DRAM 事务。 |\n| 通信 | 分设备草稿传输候选、概率或特征；目标 TP/EP 的通信随实际行数与专家集合变化。词表分片的残余质量需全局归一化，不能各片独立归一化后拼接。 |\n| 准备 | lookup、建树、目标 grammar 路径检查、父行映射、mask、packing、图桶、快照和草稿启动。路径检查仅遍历到首次非法或实际终止位置；固定形状仍须记录有效行与真实物理执行范围。 |\n| 同步 | 草稿依赖、目标实际执行范围的全层完成、接受扫描、共同提交及最后使用后回收。同请求的确认依赖不能由异步接口消除。 |\n| 驻留 | 草稿权重、实际提议记录、候选 KV、特征、快照、logits、mask 和 workspace；共享对象按并集计量，驻留可能降低最大 batch 或触发驱逐。 |\n\n对全 attention 模型，设每 token 全层 KV 字节为 BKV=2ΣₗHkv,ₗdₗbₗ，bₗ 是每元素实际存储字节，另计量化元数据。链式候选的临时 KV 约 sBKV，树按实际输入节点计；接受 n 个时，约 nBKV 进入可见历史，其余等待安全回收。root 准备另按是否新增或私有重算计量。递归快照、窗口保护和分页碎片不能混入这个理想 KV 公式。\n\n完整残余需要全 token 空间上的 p、q，但不强制永久保存所有浮点向量。保存 g 个实际提议的全分布约占 O(g|𝒱|) 元素；也可保存足以恢复实际分布的 logits/变换记录，拒绝后重算，或利用单点、稀疏 q。仅保存候选自身的 qᵢ(xᵢ) 不足以计算一般完整残余。重算必须重现原提议分布。目标可保留全部实际输入行 logits，或保留必要 hidden 后选择性计算 LM head，二者交换驻留与计算。稀疏 q 不允许忽略目标在其支持之外的残余质量。\n\n设定算例：普通每 token 10 个时间单位，γ=4 时一轮草稿 8、验证 13、管理 3、平均产出 3，则单位成本为 24/3=8，吞吐比为 10/8=1.25。batch 扩大后普通目标单位成本降为 6，同配置反而变慢；平均产出若升至 4，也仅为 24/4=6、刚好打平。这是合成计算，不是设备测量。\n\n## 预算调整与失效条件\n\n保留的是目标契约、父前缀和共同提交规则；调整的是草稿来源、γ、树宽、图桶及状态恢复策略。可以按近期接受长度分布与实际关键路径成本比较候选，在低接受收益、目标 batch 已饱和或草稿争用关键资源时降低预算或关闭投机。\n\n深度增加提高连续覆盖机会，也增加首次拒绝后的浪费；宽度增加扩大某个父路径的候选覆盖，同时增加验证行、logits 和无效分支。递归模型可能因快照写流量而偏好较浅候选，长上下文可能因 attention 读量而偏好更小树。模型、adapter、精度或目标变换改变后，需要重新验证分布身份并重新估计成本；仅接受率下降不意味着协议非法，但旧运行点可能失去收益。\n\n目标 grammar 与草稿约束差异变大时，可以让草稿采用兼容约束以减少必拒候选，或提前进行廉价路径检查；这改变的是成本和接受收益，不是要求二者必须采用相同变换。频繁接近 EOS 或输出上限时，应缩短候选预算。若使用固定图桶，仍保留真实路径边界；图多算的行不能进入分布消费或正式状态。\n\n多请求服务应比较整体 goodput、尾延迟、公平性和容量。单请求多产出几个 token，可能同时推迟其他请求或降低最大并发。固定图地址只保证存储地址稳定，不保证 row、mask、父映射及代次内容正确。故障恢复从共同确认路径继续，旧候选和旧完成通知不能重新成为历史。\n\n## 竞争解释与区分实验\n\n先固定目标采样契约、输入与输出工作量、缓存条件、到达轨迹和计时完成点。累计 kernel 时间不是端到端关键路径，相同 seed 的单条文本也不是分布参考。\n\n| 竞争解释 | 区分实验与预测 | 反证后的修正 |\n|---|---|---|\n| 草稿与目标不接近 | 对相同目标分布使用相同提议分布，理论上无拒绝；比较接受长度及草稿耗时 | 若接受长度已高而仍慢，转查验证、管理和资源争用；只换成同模型却保留不同变换不构成 p=q 对照。 |\n| 残余或提议记录错误 | 小词表精确枚举一般分布、单点 q、互斥支持及 p=q；与直接目标序列分布比较 | 缺失草稿支持之外的目标质量时修残余范围；候选 q 与记录 q 不一致时修提议身份。 |\n| 不可达 grammar 后缀被提前处理 | 目标首步只允许 a，草稿提出 b,b；严格参考在 H+b 请求分布就报错。正确回合输出 a，只消费 root 分布，K 不增加；提前处理后缀的变体必须失败 | 若确认起点本身无合法质量，保留真实失败；若只有必拒后缀失败，修路径截断和分布形成顺序，不能解除 grammar 或反复回到 S0。 |\n| 终止与接受状态混同 | 分别构造已接受且仍可继续的 grammar、接受 EOS、bonus EOS、草稿私有停止和长度停止，记录实际 q、目标输入行与分布访问 | 在 accepting 状态提前结束时修终止谓词；EOS 后继续访问分布时修停止顺序；修正/bonus 被计为已输入时修 K。 |\n| mask 错误 | 用逐路径串行前向作参考，将实际 packed 可见集送入玩具 attention；分别注入兄弟泄漏和遗漏自身 | 若 hidden、KV 或后续分布发生分歧，修可见集；集合成员检查本身只能证明局部拓扑。 |\n| logits 一步错位 | 保持正确 mask，单独将验证索引从父行改为节点行；比较每个候选之前的条件分布 | 输入行输出正确、验证分布错误时修父行映射，不能靠屏蔽自身补救。 |\n| 回滚或状态压紧错误 | 固定接受路径，将 packed 全层 KV 与该路径串行前向逐层比较；枚举 n=0 到全部接受 | token 相同而后续状态不同，应查全层收集、递归快照和窗口恢复。 |\n| 验证或 batch 饱和 | 固定请求轨迹扫描 γ、宽度和 batch，分别记录 root 准备、验证、接受产出及其他请求等待 | 小批有益而大批失效时动态减预算；接受率不是充分解释。 |\n| 准备、通信或驻留限制 | 固定候选，改变建树/图路径、草稿设备或快照方式，记录关键路径和物理容量 | 时间没有随被干预阶段变化时，不能继续把主因归给该阶段；实际事务与逻辑字节分开报告。 |\n\n配套检查以精确有理数枚举单步协议和跨回合固定长度序列；独立参考只按目标条件分布逐 token 展开，不使用投机接受结果。它还检验一般期望长度恒等式，并故意替换接受规则、残余分布和条件前缀，要求参考实际发现偏差。有序单点树协议另以直接目标概率为参考，不复用其残余选择结果。\n\ngrammar 检查另执行候选准备、目标路径检查、按需分布访问及状态提交的有限流程模型。有限语言目标参考由前缀后继表定义，非法前缀或已终止状态不提供分布；直接目标轨迹参考独立扫描可完成词串。确定性 b,b 反例要求输出 a、只访问起点分布、无目标候选输入行、K 不增加，并以闭式递归状态核对保留边界。第二位置非法、草稿停止、目标停止、接受且可继续、零候选、零预算、接受 EOS 与 bonus EOS 分别检查访问轨迹。故意提前处理全部后缀的变体必须真正触发严格参考的死前缀错误。这些流程分支检查不把固定 uniform 当作统计采样证明；概率保持由前述精确质量检查承担。\n\n树形模型检查使用两层、单头、二维的确定性 CPU 玩具前向，实际执行 Q/K/V 投影、softmax attention、残差与非线性更新。packed 版本消费实际构造的可见索引和节点到物理行映射；参考版本对每条路径重新执行普通串行 causal 前向。比较范围包括节点输出、验证候选的父行分布和逐层状态。兄弟泄漏、遗漏自身、节点行代替父行三个负对照必须产生参考可检出的差异，不能只靠插入并查询集合成员获得成功。\n\n这些检查不执行真实目标模型、CUDA、DMA、图重放、词表分布式归约或 GPU 计时，也不证明任意 grammar 编译器、随机流实现、取消同步或知识使用效果。真实设备仍需检验数值合同、完成证据、实际事务量、资源争用和性能。论文报告只支持其来源条件，不能写成本库实测。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## 来源与推导\n\n- [S-SPEC：Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding](../sources/S-SPEC.md)：固定 2211.17192v2 的 §2.2–2.3 与附录 A.1 将采样变换纳入实际 p、q，以 min(1,p/q) 接受，并在拒绝时从 norm([p−q]₊) 重采样；证明适用于任意 p、q，不要求草稿支持覆盖目标。§3.6 与 §6 将相同架构和相同 standardization 说明为实验选择。§3 的产出、墙钟与算术量公式具有 i.i.d. 接受率、可用并行资源和成本口径等假设。本章据此展开支持边界和跨回合状态，未将公式当作真实负载保证。\n  [完整原文](../../references/S-SPEC/reader.html) · [全文文本](../../references/S-SPEC/fulltext.txt)\n\n- [S-HF-CACHE：Transformers — Caching](../sources/S-HF-CACHE.md)：固定文档说明逐层复用历史 K/V、计算并合并新增 K/V，增量 mask 覆盖历史与当前输入，且当前位置表示预测下一 token。它区分 Dynamic、Static、StaticSlidingWindow 等存储形态。本章据此区分输入行、后继 logits、全层有效边界与物理容量；树拓扑和提交协议为本章推导。文档没有定义 cache_position 或 position_ids 的具体参数合同，不能据字段名猜测位置语义；其乘 mask 示意也不能替代合法 softmax 可见集合。\n  [完整原文](../../references/S-HF-CACHE/reader.html) · [全文文本](../../references/S-HF-CACHE/fulltext.txt)\n\n- [S-TORCH-REPRO：PyTorch — Reproducibility](../sources/S-TORCH-REPRO.md)：随机种子与确定性配置可以控制特定执行路径中的随机性，但不保证跨版本、平台、CPU/GPU 和全部算法路径逐位一致。本章因此以分布参考检验随机协议，并将按请求与角色组织随机子流作为设计选择，而非来源保证。\n  [完整原文](../../references/S-TORCH-REPRO/reader.html) · [全文文本](../../references/S-TORCH-REPRO/fulltext.txt)\n\n- [S-EAGLE：EAGLE](../sources/S-EAGLE.md)：固定 2401.15077v1 的 §2–4 将 LM head 前特征、提前一步的已采样 "e7e30a27ff5b42d93b55bc6e36a008ce6f5c100410fbe357ee3870a4ad601f90", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "related_chapter", "content": "# K-SYS-025：已知张力：单实例交互式服务中的机制冲突\n\n单实例交互式生成服务里，若干机制在同一资源与同一时延预算上相互拉扯。下列条目是有来源支撑的探索起点：各写机制、来源指针、可观测信号与反转条件。清单不穷尽，也不构成任务必须处理的缺陷列表。\n\n## Decode 带宽下界与 ITL\n\n机制：每轮 decode 若读取大块权重与各请求可见 KV，则设备主存流量给出迭代时间下界；客户端 ITL 还要覆盖主机、采样、同步与排队。当流量下界已接近或超过目标 ITL，继续堆叠同轮工作只会恶化间隔，吞吐与单请求间隔不能用同一除法解释。\n\n来源指针：[容量与 roofline](../hardware/memory-and-roofline.md) 中权重/KV 流量分解；[ORCA](../sources/S-ORCA.md) 的迭代批语义说明一轮推进多个请求仍共享同轮等待。\n\n可观测：活跃 decode 数增加时，每请求输出间隔近似不降或上升，而设备 HBM 字节/迭代接近表征带宽×时间；主机空闲比例高。\n\n反转：权重驻留被更高层缓存命中、KV 读取因共享或压缩真实下降、目标 ITL 放宽，或阶段分离使本实例不再承担该流量形态时，张力缓解或形态改变。\n\n## Prefill 与 Decode 干扰\n\n机制：长 prefill 的大矩阵与长上下文 attention 占用 SM、HBM 与工作区；与短 query decode 同实例交错时，decode 的迭代被拉长，表现为已有流式用户的 ITL 尖峰。分块把 prefill 切成可调度片，但每块仍须按可见性读取其依赖的历史，块长受剩余 ITL 预算约束，不是任意变小就无代价。\n\n来源指针：[SARATHI](../sources/S-SARATHI.md)；[分块 Prefill](../algorithms/chunked-prefill.md)。\n\n可观测：新长大请求到达后，无关会话的 token 间隔同步变差；时间线上出现长 prefill kernel 与 decode 交替，decode 间隙被拉宽；TTFT 改善若来自抢占 decode，常伴随 ITL 恶化。\n\n反转：prefill/decode 分实例且状态迁移成本可接受、到达以短输入为主、或时间片与准入使 decode 预算硬隔离时，同实例干扰下降。分实例引入的传输与调度是另一组成本。\n\n## 投机收益随 Batch 增大而消失\n\n机制：投机用草稿成本换目标并行验证；单位已确认 token 成本须低于普通目标 decode。连续批摊薄每 token 权重读取后，普通 decode 变便宜，同一 γ 与草稿配置可能从加速变为减速；额外验证 token 还占用其他请求预算。\n\n来源指针：[投机解码](../algorithms/speculation-budget.md)；[S-SPEC](../sources/S-SPEC.md)。设定算例见该章：普通每 token 10 单位、投机后 8 单位有益；普通降至 6 单位后同配置变慢。\n\n可观测：小批或单请求加速比明显，批增大后接受长度未变但端到端 token/s 低于关闭投机；验证占用的 token 预算挤出其他就绪请求。\n\n反转：批变小、草稿更准或更便宜、验证与 decode 能重叠且不挤占关键资源，或普通路径因别的原因变慢时，投机可再次有利。关闭投机是合法运行点。\n\n## KV 量化的质量悬崖\n\n机制：降低 KV 位宽削减驻留与部分流量，但误差经注意力传播；分组轴、尺度与残余区配置在某些位宽/模型/任务上出现断崖式质量下降，而不是平滑可插值的曲线。容量账按理想位宽估算会忽略 scale、残余与 workspace。\n\n来源指针：[KIVI](../sources/S-KIVI.md) §3–4 与适用边界；[量化契约](../algorithms/quantization-contract.md)。\n\n可观测：某位宽下任务指标相对基线突然恶化，相邻更高位宽仍接近基线；仅测 TTFT/首 token 正常，长生成后偏离；消费压缩历史的 decode 与仍用高精度缓存的 prefill 结果不一致。\n\n反转：换分组轴或残余策略、提高关键层精度、任务对误差不敏感，或长度/批使流量不再主导时，可重新评估。悬崖位置随模型与任务移动，不能当跨模型常数。\n\n## 图桶、Workspace 与 KV 争容量\n\n机制：CUDA Graph 与固定计划缓冲需要稳定地址与多桶驻留；注意力/矩阵 workspace、流水双缓冲同样占用设备容量。它们与 KV 争同一显存池：桶与 workspace 增大可降提交与峰值临时，却直接减少可驻留历史或并发。\n\n来源指针：[CUDA Graph 缓冲](../frameworks/cuda-graph-buffers.md)；[内存分配器](../system-semantics/memory-allocators.md)；[S-TORCH-CUDA](../sources/S-TORCH-CUDA.md)。\n\n可观测：启用更多图桶或更大 workspace 后，最大并发或最大上下文下降；OOM/驱逐与“计算变快”同时出现；页回收成功但图仍引用旧代次导致错误。\n\n反转：形状集合收窄使桶数下降、改为 eager 且提交不是关键路径、或扩容设备内存时，争用缓和。地址不稳定的负载下图路径本身可能先失效。\n\n## 小 Batch 下主机侧占比\n\n机制：设备迭代很短时，分词、调度、采样、JSON/协议与 launch 的主机时间在端到端中占比上升。优化仅针对 HBM 或 GEMM 时，壁钟改善受 Amdahl 限制；图与异步可摊薄提交，但不消除采样与协议成本。\n\n来源指针：[主机运行时](../system-semantics/host-runtime.md)；[CUDA 执行中的 eager/图](../hardware/cuda-execution.md)；[S-NSYS](../sources/S-NSYS.md) 对 host/device 时间线边界的说明。\n\n可观测：设备利用率低但 ITL 仍高；时间线显示 kernel 很短、host 线程或运行时跨度占间隔大部分；增大批后设备占比上升、主机占比下降。\n\n反转：批变大、输出更长使设备工作占优，或主机路径与设备重叠且无假同步时，张力形态改变。主机变快不自动提高已饱和的 HBM 路径。\n\n## 张力之间的耦合\n\n上述条目常耦合：为缓解带宽–ITL 而增大批，可能削弱投机；为保护 ITL 而切细 prefill，可能增加 launch 与主机占比；为省 KV 而量化，可能撞上质量悬崖；为降提交而加图桶，可能挤掉并发。要区分单因素效应，可以控制其他条件；要识别交互作用，则比较交叉配置或建立因素实验。改动多个机制本身合法，关键是证据能否区分各解释，不能把组合效应任意归给一项。\n\n设计轴包括：同实例混合 vs 阶段分离、批大小与准入、投机开关与 γ、KV 表示位宽、图/eager、主机/设备重叠程度。轴上未列点同样合法。\n\n## 用成本关系判断张力是否真正成立\n\n设已确认某轮必须跨设备主存读取 D=8 GiB，匹配访问形态的可持续带宽 B=0.8 TiB/s，则只计该流量的时间下界为 D/B=9.765625 ms。若本例要求这一轮完成才能生成下一个 token，且 ITL 预算为 5 ms，这个运行点不能满足预算。以上均为设定值；张量的逻辑大小不能未经计量直接当作 D，cache 命中、共享读取和压缩都可能改变实际跨边界字节。\n\n若有人将延迟归因为提交空隙，另一解释是主存流量，区分方法是保持语义与长度不变，分别改变提交组织和读取字节，记录设备事务、关键路径与完整 token 间隔。图重放降低 host 时间而设备流量和 ITL 几乎不变，支持流量主导；减少合法读取量后设备时间下降，进一步支持这个解释。干扰、频率、并发和误差也要记录；单一利用率不能排除竞争解释。\n\n这个下界约束该运行点，不规定分离服务、量化、改变批或专用 kernel 中的哪一个必须采用。若优化只是重叠同一个共享带宽上的消费者，D/B 的总流量账不会因此消失。\n\n<!-- infraagents:provenance -->\n\n## to the Proceedings of the  \n16th USENIX Symposium on Operating  \nSystems Design and Implementation  \nis sponsored by\nOrca: A Distributed Serving System for \nTransformer-Based Generative Models\nGyeong-In Yu and Joo Seong Jeong, Seoul National University;  \nGeon-Woo Kim, FriendliAI and Seoul National University; Soojeong Kim, FriendliAI; \nByung-Gon Chun, FriendliAI and Seoul National University\nhttps://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi22/presentation/yu\n\n\n--- PAGE 2 ---\nORCA: A Distributed Serving System for\nTransformer-Based Generative Models\nGyeong-In Yu\nSeoul National University\nJoo Seong Jeong\nSeoul National University\nGeon-Woo Kim\nFriendliAI\nSeoul National University\nSoojeong Kim\nFriendliAI\nByung-Gon Chun∗\nFriendliAI\nSeoul National University\nAbstract\nLarge-scale Transformer-based models trained for generation\ntasks (e.g., GPT-3) have recently attracted huge interest, em-\nphasizing the need for system support for serving models in\nthis family. Since these models generate a next token in an au-\ntoregressive manner, one has to run the model multiple times\nto process an inference request where each iteration of the\nmodel generates a single output token for the request. How-\never, existing systems for inference serving do not perform\nwell on this type of workload that has a multi-iteration char-\nacteristic, due to their inﬂexible scheduling mechanism that\ncannot change the current batch of requests being processed;\nrequests that have ﬁnished earlier than other requests in a\nbatch cannot return to the client, while newly arrived requests\nhave to wait until the current batch completely ﬁnishes.\nIn this paper, we propose iteration-level scheduling, a new\nscheduling mechanism that schedules execution at the gran-\nularity of iteration (instead of request) where the scheduler\ninvokes the execution engine to run only a single iteration of\nthe model on the batch. In addition, to apply batching and\niteration-level scheduling to a Transformer model at the same\ntime, we suggest selective batching, which applies batching\nonly to a selected set of operations. Based on these two tech-\nniques, we have implemented a distributed serving system\ncalled ORCA, with additional designs for scalability to models\nwith hundreds of billions of parameters. Our evaluation on a\nGPT-3 175B model shows that ORCA can signiﬁcantly out-\nperform NVIDIA FasterTransformer in terms of both latency\nand throughput: 36.9× throughput improvement at the same\nlevel of latency.\n1\nIntroduction\nLanguage generation tasks are becoming increasingly\nparamount to many types of applications, such as chatbot [9,\n52], summarization [41,45,54], code generation [13], and cap-\ntion generation [65,66]. Moreover, recent works published by\n∗Corresponding author.\nAI21 Labs [37], DeepMind [26,48], Google [15,21,63], Meta\nPlatforms [10,67], Microsoft [50], Microsoft & NVIDIA [59],\nand OpenAI [12] have reported that every language process-\ning task, including translation [11,17], classiﬁcation [20,53],\nquestion-answering [32, 33, 40] and more, can be cast as a\nlanguage generation problem and have shown great improve-\nments along this direction. The rise of generative models is\nnot limited to the language domain; the AI community has\nalso given growing interest to generation problems in other do-\nmains such as image, video, speech, or a mixture of multiple\ndomains [19,38,51,62]. At the heart of generative models lies\nthe Transformer architecture [60] and its variants [15,47–49].\nBy relying on the attention mechanism [60], Transformer\nmodels can learn better representations where each element\nof the sequence may have a direct connection with every other\nelement, which was not possible in recurrent models [25].\nTo use generative models in real-world applications, we\noften delegate the inference procedure to a separate service\nresponsible for ML inference serving. The growing demands\nfor this service, which should provide inference results for\nclient requests at low latency and high throughput, have fa-\ncilitated the development of inference serving systems such\nas Triton Inference Server [7] and TensorFlow Serving [42].\nThese systems can use a separately-developed DNN execution\nengine to perform the actual tensor operations. For example,\nwe can deploy a service for language generation tasks by\nusing a combination of Triton and FasterTransformer [4], an\nexecution engine optimized for the inference of Transformer-\nbased models. In this case, Triton is mainly responsible for\ngrouping multiple client requests into a batch, while Faster-\nTransformer receives the batch from Triton and conducts the\ninference procedure in the batched manner.\nUnfortunately, we notice that the existing inference sys-\ntems, including both the serving system layer and the execu-\ntion engine layer, have limitations in handling requests for\nTransformer-based generative models. Since these models are\ntrained to generate a next token in an autoregressive manner,\none should run the model as many times as the number of to-\nkens to generate, while for other models like ResNet [24] and\nUSENIX Association\n16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation    521\n\n\n--- PAGE 3 ---\nBERT [18] a request can be processed by running the model\nonce. That is, in order to process a request to the generative\nmodel, we have to run multiple iterations of the model; each\niteration generates a single output token, which is used as\nan input in the following iteration. Such multi-iteration char-\nacteristic calls into question the current design of inference\nsystems, where the serving system schedules the execution\nof the engine at the granularity of request. Under this design,\nwhen the serving system dispatches a batch of requests to\nthe engine, the engine returns inference results for the entire\nbatch at once after processing all requests within the batch.\nAs different client requests may require different numbers of\niterations for processing, requests that have ﬁnished earlier\nthan others in the batch cannot return to the client, resulting\nin an increased latency. Requests arrived after dispatching the\nbatch also should wait for processing the batch, which can\nsigniﬁcantly increase the requests’ queueing time.\nIn this paper, we propose to schedule the execution of the\nengine at the granularity of iteration instead of request. In\nparticular, the se\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 8000:24000]\n efﬁciency.\nSince the Attention operation is not associated with any model\nparameters, applying batching to Attention has no beneﬁt of\nreducing the amount of GPU memory reads by reusing the\nloaded parameters across multiple requests.\nBased on these techniques, we design and implement\nORCA, a distributed serving system for Transformer-based\ngenerative models. In order to handle large-scale models,\n1In some literature the Attention operation has an extended deﬁnition that\nincludes linear layers (QKV Linear and Attn Out Linear; Figure 1b). On the\nother hand, we use a narrow deﬁnition as described in Figure 1b.\nORCA adopts parallelization strategies including intra-layer\nand inter-layer model parallelism, which were originally de-\nveloped by training systems [55,58] for Transformer models.\nWe also devise a new scheduling algorithm for the proposed\niteration-level scheduling, with additional considerations for\nmemory management and pipelined execution across work-\ners.\nWe evaluate ORCA using OpenAI GPT-3 [12] models with\nvarious conﬁgurations, scaling up to 341B of parameters. The\nresults show that ORCA signiﬁcantly outperforms FasterTrans-\nformer [4], showing 36.9× throughput improvement at the\nsame level of latency. While we use a language model as\na driving example throughout the paper and conduct experi-\nments only on language models, generative models in other\ndomains can beneﬁt from our approach as long as the mod-\nels are based on the Transformer architecture and use the\nautoregressive generation procedure [19,38,51,62].\n2\nBackground\nWe provide background on the inference procedure of\nGPT [12,47], a representative example of Transformer-based\ngenerative models that we use throughout this paper, and ML\ninference serving systems.\nInference procedure of GPT.\nGPT is an autoregressive\nlanguage model based on one of architectural variants of\nTransformer [60]. It takes text as input and produces new text\nas output. In particular, the model receives a sequence of input\ntokens and then completes the sequence by generating subse-\nquent output tokens. Figure 1a illustrates a simpliﬁed compu-\ntation graph that represents this procedure with a three-layer\nGPT model, where nodes and edges indicate Transformer\nlayers and dependencies between the layers, respectively. The\nTransformer layers are executed in the order denoted by the\nnumbers on the nodes, and the nodes that use the same set\nof model parameters (i.e., nodes representing the same layer)\nare ﬁlled with the same color.\nThe generated output token is fed back into the model to\ngenerate the next output token, imposing a sequential, one-\nby-one inference procedure. This autoregressive procedure of\ngenerating a single token is done by running all the layers of\nthe model with the input, which is either a sequence of input\ntokens that came from the client or a previously generated out-\nput token. We deﬁne the run of all layers as an iteration of the\nmodel. In the example shown in Figure 1a, the inference pro-\ncedure comprises three iterations. The ﬁrst iteration (“iter 1”)\ntakes all the input tokens (“I think this”) at once and generates\nthe next token (“is”). This iteration composes an initiation\nphase, a procedure responsible for processing the input tokens\nand generating the ﬁrst output token. The next two iterations\n(“iter 2” and “iter 3”), which compose an increment phase,\ntake the output token of the preceding iteration and generate\n522    16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation\nUSENIX Association\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\nI think this\n1\n4\n7\n5\n8\nis\ngreat\n6\n9\nis\ngreat\n<EOS>\n2\niter 1\niter 2\niter 3\n3\n(a) A computation graph representing\nan inference procedure using a GPT\nmodel. In this case, “iter 3” is the last iteration because\nit produces “<EOS>”, a special end-of-sequence token that\nterminates output generation. Note that while the increment\nphase comprises multiple iterations because each iteration\nis only able to process a single token, the initiation phase is\ntypically implemented as a single iteration by processing all\nthe input tokens in parallel.\nThe original Transformer [60] employs two stacks of Trans-\nformer layers, while GPT’s architecture consists of a single\nlayer stack, namely decoder. Figure 1b shows a Transformer\nlayer used in GPT. Among the operations that compose the\nTransformer layer, Attention is the essence that distinguishes\nTransformer from other architectures. At a high level, the At-\ntention operation computes a weighted average of the tokens\nof interest so that each token in the sequence is aware of the\nother. It takes three inputs, query, key, and value, computes dot\nproducts of the query (for the current token) with all keys (for\nthe tokens of interest), applies Softmax on the dot products\nto get weights, and conducts weighted average of all values\nassociated with the weights.\nSince the Attention requires keys and values of all pre-\nceding tokens,2 we consider the keys and values as internal\nstates that should be maintained across multiple iterations. A\nnaïve, state-less inference procedure would take all tokens in\nthe sequence (including both the client-provided input tokens\nand the output tokens generated so far) to recompute all the\nkeys and values at every iteration. To avoid such recomputa-\ntion, fairseq [43] suggests incremental decoding, which saves\nthe keys and values for reuse in successive iterations. Other\nsystems for Transformer such as FasterTransformer [4] and\nMegatron-LM [3] also do the same.\n2Language models like GPT use causal masking, which means all pre-\nceding tokens are of interest and participate in the Attention operation.\nFigure 1c illustrates the state usage pattern of Transformer,\nalong with LSTM [25] that also maintains internal states. The\nmain difference is that the size of the states (k for Attention\nkey and v for value) in Transformer increases with iteration,\nwhereas the size of the states (c for LSTM internal memory\nand h for LSTM layer’s input/output) in LSTM remains con-\nstant. When processing the token at index t, the Attention\noperation takes all previous Attention keys kl,1:t−1 and values\nvl,1:t−1 along with the current key kl,t and value vl,t.3 There-\nfore, the Attention operation should perform computation on\ntensors of different shapes depending on the number of tokens\nalready processed.\nPrior to the Attention operation, there are the layer normal-\nization operation (LayerNorm) and the QKV Linear (linear\nand split operations to get the query, key and value). Opera-\ntions performed after Attention are, in order, a linear operation\n(Attn Out Linear), an add operation for residual connection\n(Add), layer normalization operation (LayerNorm), the multi-\nlayer perceptron (MLP) operations, and the other residual\nconnection operation (Add).\nML inference serving systems.\nGrowing demands for ML-\ndriven applications have made ML inference serving service\na critical workload in modern datacenters. Users (either the\nend-user or internal microservices of the application) submit\nrequests to an inference service, and the service gives replies\non the requests based on a pre-deﬁned ML model using its\nprovisioned resource, typically equipped with specialized ac-\ncelerators such as GPUs and TPUs. In particular, the service\nruns a DNN model with input data to generate output for the\n3kl,1:t−1 represents Attention keys of the l-th layer for tokens at indices\n1 to t −1 while kl,t is for the Attention key of the l-th layer for the token at\nindex t. Same for vl,1:t−1 and vl,t.\nUSENIX Association\n16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation    523\n\n\n--- PAGE 5 ---\nrequest\nresponse\nServing System\nEndpoint\nScheduler\nExecution\nEngine\nRequest Queue\n!\n\"\n#\n$\nx1\nx1: I think\nx2\nx2: I love\nx1\nx1: this is great\nx2\nx2: you\nFigure 2: Overall workﬂow of serving a generative language\nmodel with existing systems.\nrequest. Just like other services operating on datacenters, a\nwell-managed inference service should provide low latency\nand high throughput within a reasonable amount of cost.\nTo meet such constraints, service operators often use ML\ninference serving systems such as Triton Inference Server [7]\nand TensorFlow Serving [42]. These systems can be seen as\nan abstraction sitting atop underlying model execution en-\ngines such as TensorRT [6], TVM [14], TensorFlow [8], and\nmany others [44,46], being agnostic to various kinds of ML\nmodels, execution engines, and computing hardware. While\ndelegating the role of driving the main mathematical opera-\ntions to the engines, serving systems are in charge of exposing\nendpoints that receive inference requests, scheduling execu-\ntions of the engine, and sending responses to the requests.\nAccordingly, these systems focus on aspects such as batch-\ning the executions [7,16,35,42,56], selecting an appropriate\nmodel from multiple model variants [16,27,30,57], deploying\nmultiple models (each for different inference services) on the\nsame device [7,29,35,56], and so on.\nAmong the features and optimizations provided by serv-\ning systems, batching is a key to achieve high accelerator\nutilization when using accelerators like GPUs. When we run\nthe execution engine with batching enabled, the input tensors\nfrom multiple requests coalesce into a single, large input ten-\nsor before being fed to the ﬁrst operation of the model. Since\nthe accelerators prefer large input tensors over small ones to\nbetter exploit the vast amount of parallel computation units,\nthe engine’s throughput is highly dependent on the batch size,\ni.e., the number of inference requests the engine processes\ntogether. Reusing the model parameters loaded from off-chip\nmemory is another merit in batched execution, especially\nwhen the model involves memory-intensive operations.\nFigure 2 shows an overall workﬂow of serving a generative\nlanguage model with existing serving systems and execution\nengines. The main component of the serving system (e.g., Tri-\nton [7]) is the scheduler, which is responsible for  creating\na batch of requests by retrieving requests from a queue and \nscheduling the execution engine (e.g., FasterTransformer [4])\nto process the batch. The execution engine  processes the\nreceived batch by running multiple iterations of the model\nbeing served and  returns the generated text back to the\nserving system. In Figure 2, the serving system schedules the\nengine to process two requests (x1: “I think”, x2: “I love”) in\niter 1\nx1\nx1\nx2\nx2\niter 2\nI\nthink\nI\nlove\nthis\nyou\nthis\nyou\niter 3\nis\n-\nis\n<EOS>\ngreat\n-\niter 4\ngreat\n-\n<EOS>\n-\nFigure 3: An illustration for a case where the requests have the\nsame input length but some requests ﬁnish earlier than others.\nShaded tokens represent input tokens. “-” denotes inputs and\noutputs of extra computation imposed by the scheduling.\na batch and the engine generates “this is great” and “you” for\nrequests x1 and x2, respectively.\n3\nChallenges and Proposed Solutions\nIn this section, we describe challenges in serving Transformer-\nbased generative models and propose two techniques:\niteration-level scheduling and selective batching.\nC1: Early-ﬁnished and late-joining requests.\nOne major\nlimitation of existing systems is that the serving system and\nthe execution engine interact with each other only when (1)\nthe serving system schedules the next batch on an idle engine;\nor (2) the engine ﬁnishes processing the current batch. In\nother words, these systems are designed to schedule execu-\ntions at request granularity; the engine maintains a batch of\nrequests ﬁxed until all requests in the batch ﬁnish. This can be\nproblematic in the serving of generative models, since each\nrequest in a batch may require different number of iterations,\nresulting in certain requests ﬁnishing earlier than the others.\nIn the example shown in Figure 3, although request x2 ﬁnishes\nearlier than request x1, the engine performs computation for\nboth “active” and “inactive” requests throughout all iterations.\nSuch extra computation for inactive requests (x2 at iter 3 and\n4) limits the efﬁciency of batched execution.\nWhat makes it even worse is that this behavior prevents an\nearly return of the ﬁnished request to the client, imposing a\nsubstantial amount of extra latency. This is because the engine\nonly returns the execution results to the serving system when\nit ﬁnishes processing all requests in the batch. Similarly, when\na new request arrives in the middle of the current batch’s\nexecution, the aforementioned scheduling mechanism makes\nthe newly arrived request wait until all requests in the current\nbatch have ﬁnished. We argue that the current request-level\nscheduling mechanism cannot efﬁciently handle workloads\nwith multi-iteration characteristic. Note that this problem of\nearly-ﬁnished and late-joining requests does not occur in the\ntraining of language models; the training procedure ﬁnishes\nprocessing the whole batch in a single iteration by using the\nteacher forcing technique [64].\n524    16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation\nUSENIX Association\n\n\n--- PAGE 6 ---\nresponse\nOrca System\nEndpoint\nrequest\nScheduler\nRequest Pool\nExecution\nEngine\n!\n\"\n#\nx1\nx1 x11\nx11 x12\nx12 x13\nx13 x14\nx14\nx2\nx2 x21\nx21 x22\nx22\nx3\nx3 x31\nx31 x32\nx32\nx4\nx4 x41\nx41 x42\nx42 x43\nx43\nx1, x2, x3, x4\nx1, x2, x3, x4\n· · ·\n· · ·\nx15, x23, x33, x44\nx15, x23, x33, x44\n$\nFigure 4: System overview of ORCA. Interactions between\ncomponents represented as dotted lines indicate that the inter-\naction takes place at every iteration of the execution engine.\nxi j is the j-th token of the i-th request. Shaded tokens repre-\nsent input tokens received from the clients, while unshaded\ntokens are generated by ORCA. For example, request x1 ini-\ntially arrived with two input tokens (x11,x12) and have run\ntwo iterations so far, where the ﬁrst and second iterations gen-\nerated x13 and x14, respectively. On the other hand, request\nx3 only contains input tokens (x31,x32) because it has not run\nany iterations yet.\nS1: Iteration-level scheduling.\nTo address the above limi-\ntations, we propose to schedule executions at the granularity\nof iteration. At high level, the scheduler repeats the follow-\ning procedure: (1) selects requests to run next; (2) invokes\nthe engine to execute one iteration for the selected requests;\nand (3) receives execution results for the scheduled iteration.\nSince the scheduler receives a return on every iteration, it can\ndetect the completion of a request and immediately return its\ngenerated tokens to the client. For a newly arrived request, the\nrequest gets a chance to start processing (i.e., the scheduler\nmay select the new request to run next) after execution of\nthe currently scheduled iteration, signiﬁcantly reducing the\nqueueing delay. With iteration-level scheduling, the sched-\nuler has a full control on how many and which requests are\nprocessed in each iteration.\nFigure 4 depicts the system architecture and the overall\nworkﬂow of ORCA using the iteration-level scheduling. ORCA\nexposes an endpoint (e.g., HTTPS or gRPC) where inference\nrequests arrive at the system a\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 28000:44000]\nplementation    525\n\n\n--- PAGE 7 ---\nQKV Linear\nSplit\nAttention K/V Manager\nAttn x4\nx4\nAttn x3\nx3\nAttn x2\nx2\nAttn x1\nx1\nLayer Input\nKey\nValue\nx1 : (x11, x12, x13)\nx1 : (x11, x12, x13)\nx2 : (x21)\nx2 : (x21)\nMerge\nAttn Out \nLinear\n[7, H]\n[7, H]\n· · ·\n· · ·\n[7, H]\n[7, H]\n[2, 3H]\n[2, 3H]\n[3, 3H]\n[3, 3H]\n[1, 3H]\n[1, 3H]\n[1, 3H]\n[1, 3H]\n[7, 3H]\n[7, 3H]\n[7, H]\n[7, H]\nx32\nx32\nx41\nx41 x42\nx42 x43\nx43\nx14\nx14\nx22\nx22\nx31\nx31\n[2, H]\n[2, H]\n[3, H]\n[3, H]\n[1, H]\n[1, H]\n[1, H]\n[1, H]\nFigure 5: An illustration of ORCA execution engine running\na Transformer layer on a batch of requests with selective\nbatching. We only depict the QKV Linear, Attention, and\nAttention Out Linear operations for simplicity.\nbecause the scheduler should make a wish for the presence\nof two requests eligible for batching at the same time. The\nlikelihood further decreases exponentially as the batch size\nincreases, making it impractical to use a large batch size that\ncan pull out better throughput without compromising latency.\nS2: Selective batching.\nWe propose selective batching, a\ntechnique for batched execution that allows high ﬂexibility in\ncomposing requests as a batch. Instead of processing a batch\nof requests by “batchifying” all tensor operations composing\nthe model, this technique selectively apply batching only to a\nhandful of operations.\nThe main problem regarding batching described above is\nthat the three aforementioned cases4 correspond to irregu-\nlarly shaped input (or state) tensors, which cannot be coa-\nlesced into a single large tensor and fed into a batch opera-\ntion. In the canonical batching mechanism, at each iteration,\na Transformer layer takes a 3-dimensional input tensor of\nshape [B,L,H] generated by concatenating multiple [L,H] in-\nput tensors of requests in a batch, where B is the batch size,\nL is the number of tokens processed together, and H is the\nhidden size of the model. For example, in Figure 3, “iter 1”\n(initiation phase) takes an input tensor of shape [2,2,H] and\n“iter 2” (increment phase) takes a tensor of shape [2,1,H].\nHowever, when the scheduler decides to run an iteration on\nbatch (x1,x2,x3,x4) in Figure 4, the inputs for requests in the\ninitiation phase (x3 : [2,H] and x4 : [3,H]) cannot coalesce\ninto a single tensor of shape [B,L,H] because x3 and x4 have\ndifferent number of input tokens, 2 and 3.\nInterestingly, not all operations are incompatible with such\nirregularly shaped tensors. Operations such as non-Attention\nmatrix multiplication and layer normalization can be made to\nwork with irregularly shaped tensors by ﬂattening the tensors.\n4We use the ﬁrst case as a driving example, but the argument can be\nsimilarly applied to the other two cases.\nFor instance, the aforementioned input tensors for x3 and x4\ncan compose a 2-dimensional tensor of shape [∑L,H] = [5,H]\nwithout an explicit batch dimension. This tensor can be fed\ninto all non-Attention operations including Linear, Layer-\nNorm, Add, and GeLU operations because they do not need to\ndistinguish tensor elements of different requests. On the other\nhand, the Attention operation requires a notion of requests\n(i.e., requires the batch dimension) to compute attention only\nbetween the tokens of the same request, typically done by\napplying cuBLAS routines for batch matrix multiplication.\nSelective batching is aware of the different characteristics\nof each operation; it splits the batch and processes each re-\nquest individually for the Attention operation while applying\ntoken-wise (instead of request-wise) batching to other oper-\nations without the notion of requests. Figure 5 presents the\nselective batching mechanism processing a batch of requests\n(x1,x2,x3,x4) described in Figure 4. This batch has 7 input\ntokens to process, so we make the input tensor have a shape\nof [7,H] and apply the non-Attention operations. Before the\nAttention operation, we insert a Split operation and run the\nAttention operation separately on the split tensor for each\nrequest. The outputs of Attention operations are merged back\ninto a tensor of shape [7,H] by a Merge operation, bringing\nback the batching functionality to the rest of operations.\nTo make the requests in the increment phase can use the\nAttention keys and values for the tokens processed in previous\niterations, ORCA maintains the generated keys and values in\nthe Attention K/V manager. The manager maintains these\nkeys and values separately for each request until the scheduler\nexplicitly asks to remove certain request’s keys and values,\ni.e., when the request has ﬁnished processing. The Attention\noperation for request in the increment phase (x1 and x2) takes\nkeys and values of previous tokens (x11,x12,x13 for x1; x21 for\nx2) from the manager, along with the current token’s query,\nkey, and value from the Split operation to compute attention\nbetween the current token and the previous ones.\n4\nORCA Design\nBased on the above techniques, we design and implement\nORCA: a distributed serving system for Transformer-based\ngenerative models. We have already discussed the system\ncomponents and the overall execution model of ORCA while\ndescribing Figure 4. In this section, we answer the remaining\nissues about how to build an efﬁcient system that can scale to\nlarge-scale models with hundreds of billions of parameters.\nWe also describe the scheduling algorithm for iteration-level\nscheduling, i.e., how to select a batch of requests from the\nrequest pool at every iteration.\n4.1\nDistributed Architecture\nRecent works [12,31] have shown that scaling language mod-\nels can dramatically improve the quality of models. Hence,\n526    16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation\nUSENIX Association\n\n\n--- PAGE 8 ---\nLayer1\nLayer2\nLayer3\nLayer4\nGPU1\nGPU2\nGPU3\nGPU4\nGPU5\nGPU6\nFigure 6: An example of intra- and inter- layer parallelism. A\nvertical dotted line indicates partitioning between layers and\na horizontal line indicates partitioning within a layer.\nExecution Engine\nScheduler\nController\nControl Plane\nData Plane\nWorker 1\nGPU\nGPU\nGPU\nEngine Master\nschedule\ntokens\nController\nWorker 2\nGPU\nGPU\nGPU\ntokens\ncontrol\nmessage\ncontrol\nmessage\ntokens\nFigure 7: An illustration of the distributed architecture of\nORCA’s execution engine using the parallelization conﬁgura-\ntion shown in Figure 6. For example, the ﬁrst inter-layer parti-\ntion (Layer1 and Layer2) in Figure 6 is assigned to Worker1,\nwhile the second partition is assigned to Worker2.\nsystem support for serving such large language models is get-\nting more importance, especially when the model does not ﬁt\nin a single GPU. In such a case, one should split the model\nparameters along with the corresponding computation and\ndistribute them across multiple GPUs and machines.\nORCA composes known parallelization techniques for\nTransformer models: intra-layer parallelism and inter-layer\nparallelism. These two model parallelism strategies, which\nare also used by FasterTransformer [4], have been origi-\nnally developed for distributed training. Intra-layer paral-\nlelism [55,58] splits matrix multiplications (i.e., Linear and\nAttention operations) and their associated parameters over\nmultiple GPUs. We omit the detail about how this strat-\negy partitions each matrix multiplication. On the other hand,\ninter-layer parallelism splits Transformer layers over multiple\nGPUs. ORCA assigns the same number of Transformer layers\nto each GPU. Figure 6 illustrates an example application of\nintra- and inter- layer parallelism to a 4-layer GPT model. The\n4 layers are split into 2 inter-layer partitions, and the layers in\nthe partition are subdivided into 3 intra-layer partitions. We\nassign each partition to a GPU, using a total of 6 GPUs.\nThe ORCA execution engine supports distributed execution\nusing the techniques described above. Figure 7 depicts the\narchitecture of an ORCA engine. Each worker process is re-\nsponsible for an inter-layer partition of the model and can be\nplaced on a different machine from each other. In particular,\neach worker manages one or more CPU threads each dedi-\ncated for controlling a GPU, the number of which depends on\nthe degree of intra-layer parallelism.\nThe execution procedure of the ORCA execution engine is\nas follows. Once the engine is scheduled to run an iteration of\nthe model for a batch of requests, the engine master forwards\nthe received information about the scheduled batch to the ﬁrst\nworker process (Worker1). The information includes tokens\nfor the current iteration and a control message, which is com-\nposed of ids of requests within the batch, current token index\n(for requests in the increment phase), and number of input\ntokens (for requests in the initiation phase). The controller of\nWorker1 hands over the information received from the engine\nmaster to the GPU-controlling threads, where each thread\nparses the information and issues proper GPU kernels to its\nassociated GPU. For example, the kernel for the Attention\noperation uses the request id and the current token index to get\nthe GPU memory address of previous keys and values kept by\nthe Attention K/V manager. In the meantime, the controller\nalso forwards the control message to the controller of the next\nworker (Worker2), without waiting for the completion of the\nkernels issued on the GPUs of Worker1. Unlike Worker1, the\ncontroller of the last worker (Worker2) waits for (i.e., syn-\nchronize with) the completion of the issued GPU kernels, in\norder to fetch the output token for each request and send the\ntokens back to the engine master.\nTo keep GPUs busy as much as possible, we design the\nORCA engine to minimize synchronization between the CPU\nand GPUs. We observe that current systems for distributed\ninference (e.g., FasterTransformer [4] and Megatron-LM [3])\nhave CPU-GPU synchronization whenever each process re-\nceives control messages5 because they exchange the messages\nthrough a GPU-to-GPU communication channel – NCCL [5].\nThe exchange of these control messages occurs at every iter-\nation, imposing a non-negligible performance overhead. On\nthe other hand, ORCA separates the communication channels\nfor control messages (plus tokens) and tensor data transfer,\navoiding the use of NCCL for data used by CPUs. Figure 7\nshows that the ORCA engine uses NCCL exclusively for ex-\nchanging intermediate tensor data (represented by dashed\narrows) as this data is produced and consumed by GPUs. Con-\ntrol messages, which is used by the CPU threads for issuing\nGPU kernels, sent between the engine master and worker con-\ntrollers by a separate communication channel that does not\ninvolve GPU such as gRPC [2].\n4.2\nScheduling Algorithm\nThe ORCA scheduler makes decisions on which requests\nshould be selected and processed at every iteration. The sched-\nuler has high ﬂexibility in selecting a set of requests to com-\n5This includes various metadata such as batch size, sequence length, and\nwhether a request within the batch has ﬁnished processing.\nUSENIX Association\n16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation    527\n\n\n--- PAGE 9 ---\npose a batch, because of the selective batching technique that\nallows the engine to run any set of requests in the batched\nmanner. Now the main question left is how to select the re-\nquests at every iteration.\nWe design the ORCA scheduler to use a simple algorithm\nthat does not change the processing order of client requests;\nearly-arrived requests are processed earlier. That is, we en-\nsure iteration-level ﬁrst-come-ﬁrst-served (FCFS) property.\nWe deﬁne the iteration-level FCFS property for workloads\nwith multi-iteration characteristics as follows: for any pair\nof requests (xi,xj) in the request pool, if xi has arrived ear-\nlier than x j, xi should have run the same or more iterations\nthan xj. Note that some late-arrived requests may return ear-\nlier to clients if the late request requires a smaller number of\niterations to ﬁnish.\nStill, the scheduler needs to take into account additional\nfactors: diminishing returns to increasing the batch size and\nGPU memory constraint. Increasing the batch size trades off\nincreased throughput for increased latency, but as the batch\nsize becomes larger, the amount of return (i.e., increase in\nthroughput) diminishes. Therefore, just like other serving sys-\ntems [7,16], ORCA also has a notion of a max batch size: the\nlargest possible number of requests within a batch. The ORCA\nsystem operator can tune this knob to maximize throughput\nwhile satisfying one’s latency budget. We will discuss this in\nmore details with experiment results in Section 6.2.\nAnother factor is the GPU memory constraint. Optimiz-\ning memory usage by reusing buffers for intermediate results\nacross multiple operations is a well-known technique used by\nvarious systems [4,6], and ORCA also adopts this technique.\nHowever, unlike the buffers for intermediate results that can\nbe reused immediately, buffers used by the Attention K/V\nmanager for storing the keys and values cannot be reclaimed\nuntil the ORCA scheduler notiﬁes that the corresponding re-\nquest has ﬁnished processing. A naïve implementation can\nmake the scheduler fall into a deadlock when the scheduler\ncannot issue an iteration for any requests in the pool because\nthere is no space left for storing a new Attention key and value\nfor the next token. This requires the ORCA scheduler to be\naware of the remaining size of pre-allocated memory regions\nfor the manager.\nThe ORCA scheduler takes all these factors into account:\nit selects at most “max batch size” requests based on the ar-\nrival time, while reserving enough space for storing keys and\nvalues to a request when the request is scheduled for the ﬁrst\ntime. We describe the scheduling process in Algorithm 1. The\nalgorithm selects a batch of requests from the request pool\n(line 4) and schedules the batch (line 5). The Select function\n(line 17) selects at most max_bs requests from the pool based\non the arrival time of the request (lines 20-22). Algorithm 1\ndoes not depict the procedure of request arrival and return;\none may think of it as there exist concurrent threads insert-\ning newly arrived requests into request_pool and removing\nﬁnished requests from request_pool.\nAlgorithm 1: ORCA scheduling algorithm\nParams: n_workers: number of workers, max_bs:\nmax batch size, n_slots: number of K/V slots\n1 n_scheduled ←0\n2 n_rsrv ←0\n3 while true do\n4\nbatch,n_rsrv ←Select(request_pool,n_rsrv)\n5\nschedule engine to run one iteration of\nthe model for the batch\n6\nforeach req in batch do\n7\nreq.state ←RUNNING\n8\nn_scheduled ←n_scheduled +1\n9\nif n_scheduled = n_workers then\n10\nwait for return of a scheduled batch\n11\nforeach req in the returned batch do\n12\nreq.state ←INCREMENT\n13\nif ﬁnished(req) then\n14\nn_rsrv ←n_rsrv−req.max_tokens\n15\nn_scheduled ←n_scheduled −1\n16\n17 def Select(pool, n_rsrv):\n18\nbatch ←{}\n19\npool ←{req ∈pool|req.state ̸= RUNNING}\n20\nSortByArrivalTime(pool)\n21\nforeach req in pool do\n22\nif batch.size() = max_bs then break\n23\nif req.state = INITIATION then\n24\nnew_n_rsrv ←n_rsrv+req.max_tokens\n25\nif new_n_rsrv > n_slots then break\n26\nn_rsrv ←new_n_rsrv\n27\nbatch ←batchS{req}\n28\nreturn batch,n_rsrv\nWhen the scheduler considers a request in the initiation\nphase, meaning that the request has never been scheduled\nyet, the scheduler uses the request’s max_tokens6 attribute\nto reserve max_tokens slots of GPU memory for storing the\nkeys and values in advance (lines 23-26). The scheduler deter-\nmines whether the reservation is possible (line 25) based on\nn_rsrv, the number of currently reserved slots, where a slot\nis deﬁned by the amount of memory required for storing an\nAttention key and value for a single token. Here, n_slots is a\nparameter tuned by the ORCA system operator indicating the\nsize of memory region (in terms of slots) allocated to the At-\ntention K/V manager. Since the number of tokens in a request\ncannot exceed max_tokens, if the reservation is possible, it\nis guaranteed that the manager can allocate buffers for the\nnewly generated keys and values until the request ﬁnishe\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 48000:56000]\ner-layer and\nintra-layer communication. Since we design ORCA to fo-\ncus on Transformer-based generative models, ORCA pro-\nvides popular Transformer layers as a building block of mod-\nels including the original encoder-decoder Transformer [60],\nGPT [47], and other variants discussed in Raffel et al. [49].\nWe have also implemented fused kernels for LayerNorm,\nAttention, and GeLU operators, just like other systems for\ntraining or inference of Transformer models [1, 4, 58]. For\nexample, the procedure of computing dot products between\nAttention query and keys, Softmax on the dot products, and\nweighted average of Attention values are fused into a single\nCUDA kernel for the Attention operator. In addition, we go\none step further and fuse the kernels of the split Attention\noperators by simply concatenating all thread blocks of the\nkernels for different requests. Although this fusion makes the\nthread blocks within a kernel have different characteristics and\nlifetimes (which is often discouraged by CUDA programming\npractice) because they process tensors of different shapes, we\nﬁnd this fusion to be beneﬁcial by improving GPU utilization\nand reducing the kernel launch overhead [34,39].\nUSENIX Association\n16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation    529\n\n\n--- PAGE 11 ---\n6\nEvaluation\nIn this section, we present evaluation results to show the\nefﬁciency of ORCA.\nEnvironment.\nWe run our evaluation on Azure ND96asr\nA100 v4 VMs, each equipped with 8 NVIDIA 40-GB A100\nGPUs connected over NVLink. We use at most four VMs\ndepending on the size of the model being tested. Each VM\nhas 8 Mellanox 200Gbps HDR Inﬁniband adapters, providing\nan 1.6Tb/s of interconnect bandwidth between VMs.\nModels.\nThroughout the experiments, we use GPT [12] as a\nrepresentative example of Transformer-based generative mod-\nels. We use GPT models with various conﬁgurations, which is\nlisted in Table 1. The conﬁgurations for 13B and 175B models\ncome from the GPT-3 paper [12]. Based on these two mod-\nels, we change the number of layers and hidden size to make\nconﬁgurations for 101B and 341B models. All models have\na maximum sequence length of 2048, following the setting\nof the original literature [12]. We use fp16-formatted model\nparameters and intermediate activations for the experiments.\nWe also apply inter- and intra- layer parallelism strategies\ndescribed in Section 4.1, except for the 13B model that can ﬁt\nin a GPU. For example, the 175B model is partitioned over a\ntotal of 16 GPUs by using 2 inter-layer partitions subdivided\ninto 8 intra-layer partitions, where the 8 GPUs in the same\nVM belongs to the same inter-layer partition.\nBaseline system.\nWe compare with FasterTransformer [4],\nan inference engine that supports large scale Transformer\nmodels via distributed execution. While there exist other\nsystems with the support for distributed execution such as\nMegatron-LM [3] and DeepSpeed [1], these systems are pri-\nmarily designed and optimized for training workloads, which\nmakes them show relatively lower performance compared to\nthe inference-optimized systems.\nScenarios.\nWe use two different scenarios to drive our eval-\nuation. First, we design a microbenchmark to solely assess the\nperformance of the ORCA engine without being affected by\nthe iteration-level scheduling. In particular, we do not run the\nORCA scheduler in this scenario. Instead, given a batch of re-\nquests, the testing script repeats injecting the same batch into\nthe ORCA engine until all requests in the batch ﬁnishes, mim-\nicking the behavior of the canonical request-level scheduling.\nWe also assume that all requests in the batch have the same\nnumber of input tokens and generate the same number of\noutput tokens. We report the time taken for processing the\nbatch (not individual requests) and compare the result with\nFasterTransformer [4].\nThe second scenario tests the end-to-end performance of\nORCA by emulating a workload. We synthesize a trace of\nclient requests because there is no publicly-available request\ntrace for generative language models. Each request in the syn-\nthesized trace is randomly generated by sampling the number\nof input tokens and a max_gen_tokens attribute, where the\nnumber of input tokens plus max_gen_tokens equals to the\nmax_tokens attribute described in Section 4.2. We assume\nthat all requests continue generation until the number of gen-\nerated tokens reaches max_gen_tokens. In other words, we\nmake the model never emit the “<EOS>” token. This is be-\ncause we have neither the actual model checkpoint nor the\nactual input text so we do not have any information to guess\nthe right timing of the “<EOS>” token generation. Once the\nrequests are generated, we synthesize the trace by setting the\nrequest arrival time based on the Poisson process. To assess\nORCA’s behavior under varying load, we change the Poisson\nparameter (i.e., arrival rate) and adjust the request arrival time\naccordingly. We report latency and throughput using mul-\ntiple traces generated from different distributions for better\ncomparison and understanding of the behavior of ORCA and\nFasterTransformer.\n6.1\nEngine Microbenchmark\nWe ﬁrst compare the performance of FasterTransformer and\nthe ORCA engine using the ﬁrst scenario. We set all requests\nin the batch to have the same number of input tokens (32 or\n128) and generate 32 tokens. That is, in this set of experiments,\nall requests within the batch start and ﬁnish processing at the\nsame time. We conduct experiments using three different\nmodels: 13B, 101B, and 175B. For each model, we use the\ncorresponding parallelization strategy shown in Table 1.\nFigure 9 shows the performance of FasterTransformer and\nthe ORCA engine for processing a batch composed of the same\nrequests. In Figure 9a, the ORCA engine shows a similar (or\nslightly worse) performance compared to FasterTransformer\nacross all conﬁgurations. This is because ORCA does not\napply batching to the Attention operations, while FasterTrans-\nformer apply batching to all operations. Still, the performance\ndifference is relatively small. Despite not batching the Atten-\ntion operation, the absence of model parameters in Attention\nmakes this decision has little impact on efﬁciency as there\nis no beneﬁt of reusing model parameters across multiple\nrequests.\nFigure 9b presents similar results for the 101B model that\nuses all of the 8 GPUs in a single VM. From these results, we\ncan say that the ORCA engine and FasterTransformer have\ncomparable efﬁciencies in the implementations of CUDA\nkernels and the communication between intra-layer partitions.\nNote that FasterTransformer cannot use a batch size of 8 or\nlarger with the 13B model (16 or larger with the 101B model)\nbecause of the ﬁxed amount of memory pre-allocation for\neach request’s Attention keys and values, which grows in\nproportion to the max sequence length of the model (2048\nfor this case). In contrast, ORCA avoids redundant memory\n530    16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation\nUSENIX Association\n\n\n--- PAGE 12 ---\n1\n2\n4\n8\n16\n32\nBatch Size\n0\n500\n1000\n1500\nExecution Time (ms)\nft(32)\norca(32)\nft(128)\norca(128)\n(a) 13B model, 1 GPU.\n1\n2\n4\n8\n16\n32\nBatch Size\n0\n1000\n2000\nExecution Time (ms)\nft(32)\norca(32)\nft(128)\norca(128)\n(b) 101B model, 8 GPUs.\n1\n2\n4\n8\n16\n32\nBatch Size\n0\n1000\n2000\n3000\nExecution Time (ms)\nft(32)\norca(32)\nft(128)\norca(128)\n(c) 175B model, 16 GPUs.\nFigure 9: Execution time of a batch of requests using FasterTransformer and the ORCA engine without the scheduling component.\nLabel “ft(n)” represents results from FasterTransformer processing requests with n input tokens. Since each RNN cell always performs the\nexact same computation, BatchMaker can execute multiple\nRNN cells in a batched manner regardless of the position (i.e.,\ntoken index) of the cell. By doing so, BatchMaker allows a\nnewly arrived request for RNN to join (or a ﬁnished request\n532    16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation\nUSENIX Association\n\n\n--- PAGE 14 ---\nto leave) the current executing batch without waiting for the\nbatch to completely ﬁnish.\nHowever, BatchMaker cannot make batches of cells for\nTransformer models because there are too many distinct cells\n(a subgraph that encapsulates the computation for processing\na token; Figure 1c) in the graph. Each cell at a different to-\nken index t must use a different set of Attention Keys/Values.\nAs the cell for each t is different, the graph comprises L dif-\nferent cells (L denotes the number of input and generated\ntokens), signiﬁcantly lowering the likelihood of cells of the\nsame computation being present at a given moment (e.g., in\nFigure 10, L ranges from 33 = 32 + 1 to 640 = 512 + 128).\nThus execution of the cells will be mostly serialized, making\nBatchMaker fall back to non-batched execution. BatchMaker\nalso lacks support for large models that require model and\npipeline parallelism.\nWhile BatchMaker is geared towards detecting and aligning\nbatch-able RNN cells, our key principle in designing ORCA is\nto perform as much computation as possible per each round of\nmodel parameter read. This is based on the insight that reading\nparameters from GPU global memory is a major bottleneck\nin terms of end-to-end execution time, for large-scale models.\nAdhering to this principle, we apply iteration-level scheduling\nand selective batching to process all “ready” tokens in a single\nround of parameter read, regardless of whether the processing\nof tokens can be batched (non-Attention ops) or not (Attention\nops).\nSpecialized execution engines for Transformer models.\nThe outstanding performance of Transformer-based models\nencourages the development of inference systems specialized\nfor them. FasterTransformer [4], LightSeq [61], TurboTrans-\nformers [22] and EET [36] are such examples. Each of these\nsystems behave as an backend execution engine of existing\nserving systems like Triton Inference Server [7] and Tensor-\nFlow Serving [42]. That is, these systems delegate the role\nof scheduling to the serving system layer, adhering to the\ncanonical request-level scheduling. Instead, ORCA suggests\nto schedule executions at a ﬁner granularity, which is not pos-\nsible in current systems without changing the mechanism for\ncoordination between the scheduler and the execution engine.\nNote that among these systems, FasterTransformer is the only\none with the support for distributed execution. While systems\nlike Megatron-LM [3] and DeepSpeed [1] can also be used for\ndistributed execution, these systems are primarily optimized\nfor large-scale training rather than inference serving.\nInterface between serving systems and execution engines.\nCurrent general-purpose serving systems such as Triton In-\nference Server [7] and Clipper [16] serve as an abstraction\nfor handling client requests and scheduling executions of the\nunderlying execution engines. This approach is found to be\nbeneﬁcial by separating the design and implementation of\nthe serving layer and the execution layer. However, we ﬁnd\nthat the prevalent interface between the two layers is too re-\nstricted for handling models like GPT [12], which has the\nmulti-iteration characteristic. Instead, we design ORCA to\ntightly integrate the scheduler and the engine, simplifying the\napplication of the two proposed techniques: iteration-level\nscheduling and selective batching. While in this paper we\ndo not study a general interfac", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 6565], [8000, 24000], [28000, 44000], [48000, 56000], [64000, 68000]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 90276, "selected_characters": 50565, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-SARATHI/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "388df7fbaf41f3838c2005a2cfa7e27c56140abd6fd85b1c91c5bd31796ef5a3", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 8000:32000]\nmain contributions of our paper include:\n1. Chunked-prefills which allows the construction of work\nunits that are compute saturating and uniform.\n2. Decode-maximal batching which allows inefficient de-\ncodes to ‘piggyback’ with efficient prefills.\n3. Application of chunked-prefills and decode-maximal\nbatching to pipeline parallelism to significantly reduce\npipeline bubbles.\n4. Extensive evaluation over multiple models, hardware,\nand parallelism strategies demonstrating up to 1.91×\nimprovement in throughput.\n2\nBackground\nWe first give an overview of the transformer architecture,\nfollowed by a brief discussion of the two phases of LLM\ninference, and pipeline parallelism.\n2.1\nThe Transformer architecture\nFigure 2 shows the architecture of a transformer decoder\nblock. Each decoder block consists of two primary modules:\nself-attention and feed-forward network (FFN). These mod-\nules can be divided into the following six operations: pre-\nproj, attn, postproj (within the attention module), and ffn_ln1,\nffn_ln2 (within FFN) and others (e.g., layer normalization,\nactivation functions, residual connections etc.).\n2.2\nThe prefill and decode phases\nTransformer inference begins with the prefill phase that pro-\ncesses all the input tokens of a given batch in parallel. In\nthis phase, the input to a transformer block is a tensor X of\nshape [B,L,H] where B denotes the batch size, L denotes the\n2\n\n\n--- PAGE 3 ---\nLayer Norm\nAdd\nLayer Norm\nAttention\nFFN\nAdd\nWQ,K,V [H->3H]\nWO[H->H]\nDropout\nConcat\nSelf Attention\nQ\nK\nV\nAttention\n(a)\n(b)\nW [H->H2]\nW [H2->H]\nDropout\nGeLU\n(c)\nFFN\nDecoder\nBlock\nFigure 2: High-level architecture of a decoder block.\nsequence length of each request (i.e., the number of input\ntokens in the given query), and H is the model’s embedding\nsize (e.g., 5120 for LLaMA-13B).\nTable 1 shows the shapes of input, output, and weight ten-\nsors of the various operations. Each transformer block first\ncomputes self-attention on a given input X. Typically, multi-\nhead attention is used, but we consider only one head for\nsimplicity of exposition. A linear transformation preproj over\nX (using the weight tensors W Q, W K and WV of shape [H,H])\nproduces the Q, K and V that are commonly known as queries,\nkeys, and values, each of shape [B,L,H]. Internally, preproj\nis a single matrix-matrix multiplication of X with a combined\nweight tensor of shape [H,3H].\nNext, the attn computation over Q, K and V produces a\ntensor Y of shape [B,L,H]. Finally, postproj applies a lin-\near transformation over Y (using weight matrix Wo of shape\n[H,H]), returning a tensor Z of shape [B,L,H].\nNext, the FFN module performs two batched matrix-matrix\nmultiplications. In ffn_ln1, Z is multiplied with a weight ten-\nsor of shape [H,H2] producing an output tensor of shape\n[B,L,H2], which is then multiplied by a weight tensor of shape\n[H2,H] in ffn_ln2 to output a tensor of shape [B,L,H]. Here,\nH2 refers to the second hidden dimension of the model.\nThe decode phase performs the same operations as prefill,\nbut only for the single token which was generated in the\nlast autoregressive iteration. Thus, the input tensor in decode\nphase is of shape [B,1,H] (as opposed to [B,L,H] of prefill).\nFurther, the attention computation for each new token depends\non the key (K), and value (V) tensors of all prior tokens in the\nsame request. To avoid recomputing K and V of all tokens in\nevery iteration, most implementations cache these values in\nGPU memory - which is referred to as the KV cache. Note\nthat each token’s K and V tensors are of shape [1,H].\n2.3\nMulti-GPU LLM Inference\nAs the model sizes of LLMs increase, it becomes neces-\nsary to scale them to multi-GPU as well as multi-node de-\nployments [19,39]. Furthermore, LLM inference throughput,\nOperation\nShapes of tensors\nInput(s)\nWeight(s)\nOutput(s)\npreproj\n[B,L,H]\n[H,H]\n[B,L,H]\nattn\n[B,L,H]\n-\n[B,L,H]\npostproj\n[B,L,H]\n[H,H]\n[B,L,H]\nffn_ln1\n[B,L,H]\n[H,H2]\n[B,L,H2]\nffn_ln2\n[B,L,H2]\n[H2,H]\n[B,L,H]\nTable 1: Shapes of the input, weight, and output tensors in\na transformer decoder block. B, L and H denote batch size,\nembedding (aka hidden) size and sequence length (L=1 during\ndecode, except for attention).\nspecifically that of the decode phase is limited by the max-\nimum batch size we can fit on a GPU. Inference efficiency\ncan therefore benefit from model-parallelism which shards\nthe model weights across multiple GPUs freeing up mem-\nory to support larger batch sizes. Prior work has employed\nboth tensor-parallelism (TP) [43] (within node) and pipeline-\nparallelism (PP) [6,46,48] (across nodes) for this purpose.\nTP shards each layer across the participating GPUs. This\nsplits both the model weights and KV cache equally across\nGPU workers, leading to linear scaling of per-GPU batch size.\nHowever, it comes at a high communication cost due to two\nall-reduce operations per layer – one in attention computation\nand the other in FFN [43]. Moreover, since these commu-\nnication operations are in the critical path, TP is preferred\nonly within a single node connected by high bandwidth in-\nterconnects like NVLink. PP is primarily used to facilitate\ncross-node deployments for very large models, where the\nmodel cannot fit within a single node.\nCompared to TP, PP splits a model layer-wise, where each\nGPU is responsible for a subset of layers. To keep all GPUs in\nthe ‘pipeline’ busy, micro-batching is employed. These micro-\nbatches move along the pipeline from one stage to the next at\neach iteration. PP has the advantage of a much better compute-\ncommunication ratio compared to TP, as we only need to send\nactivations once for multiple layers of compute. Furthermore,\nPP requires communication only via point-to-point communi-\ncation operation, compared to the more expensive allreduces\nrequired in TP. Thus, PP is the only viable model-parallelism\napproach when high-bandwidth connectivity like NVlink is\nunavailable at cluster-scale. In such settings, the use of PP\ncan help increase the maximum batch size supported in each\nnode by 2-3×, thereby improving LLM inference efficiency.\n3\nMotivation\nIn this section, we show that LLM inference is inefficient for\ntwo main reasons: (1) the decoding phase is memory-bound,\nand (2) the use of pipeline parallelism leads to significant\npipeline bubbles for LLMs. Together, these factors lead to\npoor GPU utilization for LLM inference.\n3\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\n1\n2\n4\n8\n12\n18\nBatch size\n0.00\n0.05\n0.10\n0.15\n0.20\n0.25\nTime (ms)\nPrefill\n1\n2\n4\n8\n12\n18\nBatch size\n0\n10\n20\n30\n40\nTime (ms)\nDecode\npreproj\nattn\npostproj\nffn_ln1\nffn_ln2\nothers\nFigure 3: Per-token prefill and decode time with different\nbatch sizes (sequence length = 1024) for LLaMa-13B on\nA6000 GPU. Prefill saturates GPU compute even at batch\nsize of 1 and results in almost constant per-token time across\nbatch sizes. Decode under-utilizes GPU compute and costs\nas much as 200× prefill for batch size 1. The incremental\ncost of linear operators for decode is almost zero as batch size\nincreases. The attention cost does not benefit from batch size\nas it is memory-bound.\n3.1\nAnalyzing Prefill and Decode Throughput\nFigure 3 shows the per-token cost of each of the six trans-\nformer operations (§2.1) for prefill and decode at various\nbatch sizes for a fixed sequence length (prefill+decode) of\n1024. First, we observe that prefill has almost constant per-\ntoken cost across various batch sizes, indicating that prefill\nsaturates the GPU even at batch size of 1. Second, we see\nthat decode behaves very differently from prefill as the per-\ntoken cost reduces significantly when the batch size increases.\nThird, we see that the decode cost per-token is 200×, 100×,\nand 16.7× that of prefill at batch size of 1, 2 and 18, respec-\ntively. Thus, it is clear that optimizing decodes is critical for\nefficient LLM inference. Finally, we see that the operations\nunder others contribute less than 5% of the overall runtime of\nthe transformer block. Hence, we focus on only optimizing\nthe five major operations and ignore others.\nFigure 4a shows the throughput of the prefill and decode\nstages for different batch sizes (B) and sequence lengths (L).\nWe observe that the throughput of the prefill phase saturates\nat about 180 tokens/millisecond when B × L ≥512: e.g., a\nsingle prefill request can achieve peak throughput at L ≥512.\nIn contrast, the decode throughput increases linearly with\nsmall batch sizes.
